  ## 1. Property test of savepoints in the core
  `TestTx_Savepoint_random` is in `internal/core/tx_test.go`, and `mtx.clone` in
  `internal/core/model_test.go` is the model's savepoint.
  - **What a seed runs:** 2 to 6 write transactions of up to 30 random steps:
    - creates and drops of `t1/T1`, `t2/T2`, `tX/Tx`, in two shapes;
    - upserts and deletes of keys 1 to 4;
    - annotations outside a savepoint;
    - savepoints, releases and rollbacks to a savepoint, also where the core
  must refuse them with `ErrInvalid`.

    A savepoint still open at the end is rolled back to. Then one transaction in
  four rolls back and the rest commit.
  - **What it checks:**
    - after each step, what the transaction reads equals the model;
    - while a transaction is open, the instance serves the committed state;
    - after a commit, the commit object equals the model's net effect plus the
  annotations, or is absent when the effect is empty;
    - at the end of each seed, a strict replay of the log in the model and a
  replay into an empty cache both equal the model.
  - **Size and replay:** 400 seeds in about 2 s; 40 under `-short`, `-race` or
  the address sanitizer, without checking the minimums. One seed replays with
  the core's existing flag: `go test -run '^TestTx_Savepoint_random$' -v
  ./internal/core/ -replay N`, and a failing seed prints that command.
  - **What 400 seeds reach** (the test pins a quarter of each):

  | Case | Count |
  | --- | --- |
  | Drop then create of one name with a rollback to a savepoint between | 28 |
  | First touch of a key, undone by a rollback to a savepoint | 168 |
  | Drop inside a savepoint of a table that existed before it | 263 |
  | Rollback to a savepoint in which nothing changed | 580 |
  | Savepoints still open at the end | 552 |
  | Commits | 1,179 |

  - Two hand-made mutants of `Tx.RollbackTo` each failed more than 100 seeds.

  ## 2. The engine's simulation with transactions
  The changes are in `internal/engine/simulation_test.go`.
  - **Workload:** one turn of a caller in four is `run.transaction`, in both
  workloads. One in four of those is read-only. It has 2 to 6 statements of the
  run's sequence, and each call has a timeout. It ends with a commit, or one
  time in four with a roll back.
  - **Open question with the product owner:** a statement refused for want of
  the lease, or by a stop, ends the transaction early with a roll back. So no
  caller leaves a write transaction open, and the workload never meets the case
  of an idle write transaction that keeps a lost lease from being taken back.
  - **The checks:**
    - **Commit contents:** a commit holds the statements of one call. For a
  transaction, it holds exactly its statements that succeeded and write, in
  order, with their arguments, and only if its caller called the commit and it
  succeeded, had an unknown outcome, or did not return. A transaction whose net
  effect is nothing must not be in the log; SQLite judges that, and the
  acknowledged-write check skips such transactions.
    - **No overlap:**
      - a committed transaction's parent must be the state its statements saw,
  so no commit landed while it was open;
      - `run.verifyOverlaps` uses a clock of events per engine generation: no
  write transaction's begin returns while another is open, and no single write
  starts and commits inside one.
    - **Results** (`run.verifyTxs`): all statements of a transaction saw one
  state, which holds every write acknowledged before the begin. A fresh SQLite
  runs the log up to that state, adds `pragma query_only = 1` for a read-only
  transaction, then runs the transaction's statements in order and compares
  every answer, including writes that succeeded.
    - **Unknown outcomes:** a commit of unknown outcome may be in the log or
  not, as for a statement of its own.
  - **Pinned minimums for each seed:** commits of more than one statement 0.4
  (default) and 0.6 (hammer), against 1.48 and 2.49 over 1,000 seeds. There are
  new minimums for the transactions by kind and by how they ended.
  - **1,000 seeds of each workload:** no violation, 75 s.

  | Workload | Transactions | Commits of more than one statement |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Default | 14,176 | 1,481 |
  | Hammer | 30,012 | 2,493 |

  ## 3. Planted bugs of transactions
  - **(a) cannot be found with slice 1, so it is replaced by `keepFailed`.** I
  planted (a) as asked, as `skipRollbackTo`, and 100 seeds found nothing. No
  statement of slice 1 fails after it has changed anything: an insert checks its
  table, column and key before its one row, create and drop check their names
  first, the context is checked before the write, and nothing yields to the
  scheduler between the write and the annotation. So I removed it and planted
  the nearest one: `keepFailed`, which skips the rollback to the savepoint and
  keeps the failed statement, annotated, as if it had succeeded.
  - **(b) is `readNewest`**, in `Conn.ready`: a read-only transaction moves on
  to the newest state before each statement.
  - **(c) is `rollbackKeeps`**, in `Conn.Rollback`: a write transaction with one
  write commits on its roll back.

  Each bug is found by a violation of its own (200 seeds each):

  | Bug | Seeds that found it | Its own violation |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `keepFailed` | 191 | the commit holds other statements than its
  transaction's that succeeded and write |
  | `readNewest` (hammer workload) | 185, 168 of them by its own violation | a
  statement's answer differs from SQLite's on the state of the transaction's
  beginning |
  | `rollbackKeeps` | 110 | a transaction in the log that its caller rolled back
  |

  Every seed that found a bug found nothing without it. Two old bugs got extra
  symptoms in their lists of violations: `skipUnique` (a failed insert inside a
  transaction) and `keepRead` (rows of a query in a transaction stay open).

✻ Brewed for 14s · done 6:01 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I did both items on branch `close-handover`, which starts at `origin/main`
  1cb26dc (it contains 125f5f7). There are three commits: 832c99c (code and
  tests), d74bf1e (the todo entry) and dd115b8 (the diary). Nothing is pushed
  and no pull request is open. `make test`, `make lint` and `go build -tags
  s4ql_browser ./...` pass. `go test -short -race -count=30 -run
  'TestRows/should_count_rows|TestClose/should_(free_the_connection_when|hand)'
  .` passes. I added no hook to `internal/engine`, and no test sleeps or skips.

  **What changed**
  - `/conn.go`: `conn.leave` now closes the rows through a new `conn.closeShut`.
    - `closeShut` unlocks each row's `r.mu` in a `defer`.
    - If a close panics, a deferred function frees the engine connection
  (`freeLocked`) and the panic continues. The rows queued after the one that
  panicked go back on `conn.shut`, so the next call closes them; `conn.Close`
  counts as such a call.
  - `/rows.go`: `rows.shut` frees the connection in a `defer`.
  - One extra fix the review found, which was there before PR #63:
    - A panic in the engine's `Rows.Close` skipped `held--`. The connection then
  counted the rows open for good: `IsValid` stayed false, and a close of the
  connection waiting on those rows (`closeLater`) never ran.
    - That count and close moved to a new `conn.release`, which `rows.close`
  calls in a `defer`.
  - Test helpers:
    - `panickyLog` (`/rows_test.go`) gained `at(f)`, which calls a test function
  at the engine's log event "statement".
    - `openLogged` now takes the host and moved to `/conn_test.go`.
    - New in `/conn_test.go`: `turnHost`/`turnSemaphore`, which call a test
  function when a connection's semaphore "turn of the connection" is acquired.
  - `/docs/todo.md`: the CI `browser` hang entry is under "Tests of the storage
  core", the closest section to tests and CI.

  **How each test forces its case**
  - **`TestClose` "should hand the connection to a statement that comes while
  the close of the database closes rows of the connection, and answer it as
  closed"** (the hand-over):
    1. Rows on a `sql.Conn` are read to their first row, so an engine loop is
  running.
    2. `db.Close()` starts in a goroutine. `shut` holds the connection, and its
  close of the rows ends the loop. The loop's task logs the query event, and the
  log hook blocks it there.
    3. With `turnHost` armed, a query starts on the same `sql.Conn`. The test
  waits until that query reaches `Acquire` on "turn of the connection", meaning
  it is waiting in `enter` with `waiting` set.
    4. The test releases the log. The query must get `ErrStopped` with "s4ql:
  the instance has stopped: closed". That answer is the same whichever of the
  query and the instance close goes first, because the connector is marked
  closed before `shut` runs.
    5. `db.Close()` must return nil, and the rows and the connection then close.
  - **`TestClose` "should free the connection when the close of rows that the
  close of the database left to a statement panics, and raise the panic"**
  (panic on the `leave` path):
    1. Rows on a `sql.Conn` are left unread, so their close logs the query event
  on whichever goroutine closes them.
    2. An insert on the same connection is held at its own log event, which is
  inside `enter`/`leave`. The hook then arms the log to panic at the next event.
    3. `db.Close()` finds the connection busy, leaves the rows to the insert,
  and returns nil.
    4. The test releases the insert. Its `leave` closes the rows, and the panic
  comes out of `conn.ExecContext`, where the test recovers it.
    5. `rows.Next`, `rows.Close` and `conn.Close` must then return, and a new
  instance must see both rows.
    - Before the fix this test hung: "panic: test timed out after 1m0s", in
  `rows.Next` at `/rows.go:125` (`r.mu.Lock`).
  - **`TestRows` "should count rows whose close panics as closed, so that their
  connection can go back to the pool"**: through `conn.Raw`, it closes unread
  driver rows while the log's panic is armed, then checks `IsValid()`. It failed
  before the fix ("rows_test.go:382: Not true").

  **Removing the hand-over**
  With `c.turn.Release(ctx)` removed from the waiting branch of `freeLocked`,
  `go test -short -race -count=1 -timeout 60s -run 'TestClose/should_hand' .`
  failed with "panic: test timed out after 1m0s". The query was stuck in
  `conn.enter` at `/conn.go:241`, called from `QueryContext` at `/conn.go:322`.
  I restored the line with `git checkout conn.go` and the test passes again.

  **Decision for you: a panic in `rows.shut` still escapes `db.Close()`.**
  - A panic in the close of rows by `connector.Close` (the `shut` path) now
  frees the connection, but it still comes out of `db.Close()`. It skips the
  other open rows and `c.db.Close`, so the instance stays open and the lease
  runs until it expires.
  - database/sql then treats the database as closed, so a second `db.Close()`
  does nothing.
  - For the same reason, no test covers the `defer` in `shut`: such a test
  cannot end with the instance closed, and the world's check for a leftover
  `-wal` file would fail.
  - `connector.Close` already logs a panic from a loop it ends and does not
  raise it. Recovering a panic from `shut` the same way would let the instance
  close.
  - I did not do this because your brief said to let the panic go on. Should
  `connector.Close` recover it?

  **Other review findings**
  The code-review (medium) found three things:
  - The missing `held--` on a panic: fixed, as above.
  - The `shut` panic escaping `db.Close()`: the decision above.
  - A nit, left as is: rows put back on `conn.shut` after a panic wait for the
  next call on their connection.

  **The change, in `/conn.go`:**
  - A new `connector.shut(ctx, r)` calls `rows.shut` and recovers a panic. It
  logs the event `panic` at Error with the value as "panic" and `debug.Stack()`
  as "stack", as the loop's task does. It returns `fmt.Errorf("%w: the query
  panicked: %v", engine.ErrInternal, v)`, the wording of the existing error for
  a panic of a query.

  rows and the close of the instance. It logs `close` at Info and returns that
  error through `publicError`.
  - **Decision for you:** `connector.Close` had no case of several errors, so
  you said "join or prefer". I chose to prefer: if the close of the instance
  also fails, `db.Close()` returns that error, since it says the lease may not
  be released, and the panic is only in the log.

  goroutine of the call, after the connection is freed.

  `rows.shut` (`/rows.go`) now describe this.

  rows by the close of the database panics, log the panic, and give it as an
  internal error", in `/conn_test.go`.
  - It opens two sets of rows that nothing reads, so their close by `db.Close()`
  logs the query's event on the `db.Close()` goroutine.
  - The log hook panics once, at whichever set closes first, then clears itself.
  The other set closes normally, so the result does not depend on the map
  order.
  - It checks that:
    - `db.Close()` does not panic and returns the error above.
    - Exactly one `panic` event is logged at Error with the stack, and `close`
  is logged at Info.
    - Both sets of rows then give `ErrStopped` and close cleanly.
    - A new instance sees the row, and the world's cleanup finds no `-wal` left.
  - It failed before the change: "conn_test.go:1387: Not true / a panic of the
  test", because the panic came out of `db.Close()`.
  - My first version read one set of rows and armed the panic for every event.
  That could log two panics, one recovered by the loop's task, in an order the
  map decides, so I changed it to the version above.

⏺ fabrik:builder(Fail idle transaction on lost lease) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ A builder has started on it: the instance fails an idle open write transaction
  when it loses its lease, and takes the lease back. A commit that is already
  under way is left alone.

  The spec needs one sentence changed to match, and that needs your agreement on
  the wording.

  Today: "A write transaction that is already open commits only if its manifest
  swap still succeeds."

  Proposed: "A commit that is under way succeeds only if its manifest swap still
  succeeds. A write transaction that is open and not committing fails: it is
  rolled back, and its statements and its commit get the same temporary error,
  so that it does not keep the instance from getting the lease again."

  Okay to write it so?

✻ Sautéed for 52s · done 7:31 PM

⏺ Update(docs/spec.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      41  - **The holder keeps the lease.** While the holder keeps renewi
          ng the lease, no other instance can take it. Another instance g
          ets the lease only after the holder has released it or it has t
          imed out.
      42  - **The lease times out when the manifest stands still.** The m
          anifest records how long the lease lasts, and every renewal cha
          nges it by writing a new random value. Another instance takes t
          he lease only after it has watched the manifest stay unchanged
          for longer than that, by its own monotonic clock. No instance c
          ompares its clock with another's. An instance that starts after
           a crash therefore waits at least one lease duration before it
          serves.
      43  - **Clocks affect only reads.** Before a read, the holder check
          s that less than the lease's duration has passed since it sent
          its last renewal, by its wall clock and by its monotonic clock,
           and both must show it. If an instance measures time wrongly, a
           read can miss the commits of a newer holder. Writes are never
          affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap.
      44 -- **An instance without a valid lease refuses and keeps trying.
         -** Reads and new write transactions fail at once with a tempora
         -ry error. A write transaction that is already open commits only
         - if its manifest swap still succeeds. The instance keeps trying
         - to get the lease, catches up its cache when it gets it, and th
         -en serves again. This holds at startup, when S3 cannot be reach
         -ed, and while another instance holds the lease, so a spare inst
         -ance is a normal state.                                        
      44 +- **An instance without a valid lease refuses and keeps trying.
         +** Reads and new write transactions fail at once with a tempora
         +ry error. A commit that is under way succeeds only if its manif
         +est swap still succeeds. A write transaction that is open and n
         +ot committing fails: it is rolled back, and its statements and 
         +its commit get the same temporary error, so that it does not ke
         +ep the instance from getting the lease again. The instance keep
         +s trying to get the lease, catches up its cache when it gets it
         +, and then serves again. This holds at startup, when S3 cannot 
         +be reached, and while another instance holds the lease, so a sp
         +are instance is a normal state.                                
      45  - **Opening does not wait for the lease.** Opening a database f
          ails only on local problems. A separate call waits until the in
          stance serves.
      46  - **Reads come from the local cache.** While an instance holds
          the lease, it reads from its local cache without contacting S3.
           Writes go to S3.
      47  - **The local cache is a SQLite database.** It holds the same t
          ables and rows as the database, and the engine uses it as its l
          ocal row store. S3 holds the truth in our own format, and the c
          ache can be rebuilt from it at any time.

⏺ Agent(Survey groundwork for more types) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Slice 4 is the other types then: integer, real, text and blob, still one
  column that is the primary key. A research agent is mapping what each layer
  needs and what SQLite does, so I can split it into tasks.

  One design question is yours before I define it.

  Which values does a column take that are not of its own type?

  SQLite's STRICT tables still convert on insert: the text '1' goes into an int
  column as 1, the integer 1 goes into a text column as '1', and only what
  cannot be converted is an error.

  1. Exact types, plus an integer into a real column (my recommendation). An int
     column takes integers, text takes text, blob takes blobs, real takes reals
     and integers. Everything else is rejected. It fits how the roadmap already
     leaves SQLite's leniency behind, and it can be loosened later without
     breaking anyone.
  2. Everything SQLite converts. Fully compatible, but it brings back the
     string-to-number guessing that STRICT only half removes, and it means
     reproducing SQLite's number parsing and float printing bit for bit.

  A note on budget: usage is at 85% and the lease fix is still being built. I'll
  define slice 4 and start it, and stop at your 90% until the reset on Friday
  21:00 unless you raise it.

✻ Baked for 1m 14s · done 7:36 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Findings: what exists today for the other STRICT column types

  The repository has no other changes: the scratch probe was deleted and `git
  status` is clean.

  **1. Documents**
  - `docs/roadmap.md` Restrictions:
    - "No `ANY` column type."
    - "UTF-8 only. … text values are always valid UTF-8 … a parameter or an
  expression that would produce text that is not valid UTF-8 [is rejected]."
    - "No NUL byte in SQL text."
  - Slice 1: "`select *` returns rows in primary-key order". `integer` spelled
  out is rejected.
  - Slice 2 rejects arguments that are "nil, a string, a float, a bool, a
  `[]byte`, a `time.Time`".
  - `docs/spec.md`: storage formats are "drafts until version 1 … carries
  version 0", and a commit object is too large over 5 GiB. Nothing about NaN,
  infinity, value sizes or conversion.
  - `docs/todo.md` has these open items:
    - Line 32: "Open: whether a text value may hold a NUL byte."
    - Line 34: whether rows come in SQLite's order without `ORDER BY`.
    - Line 207: "Open: whether `integer` as a declared type becomes a second
  value of `Type` … The slice that adds `integer` decides."
  - On conversion, `internal/format/README.md:79` says: "for an `INT` column,
  SQLite converts the text `'1'` and the real `1.0` to an integer … The storage
  core therefore checks the storage class itself."

  **2. Commit format** (`proto/s4ql/v1/commit.proto`)
  - `Value` is a oneof of `sint64 integer`, `double real`, `string text`
  ("always valid UTF-8"), `bytes blob` and `Null null`.
  - `CreateTable` holds `columns` (each a name and a `Type`) and `primary_key`
  (positions in key order).
  - A key is `PrimaryKey { repeated Value values }`.
  - The only integer-only part is `enum Type { TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0; TYPE_INT =
  1; }`.
  - `internal/format/check.go` `checkValue` refuses NaN ("is a real that is
  NaN") and invalid UTF-8. It allows NUL in text, ±Inf and −0;
  `reader_test.go:82` covers these.
  - The format does not check a value against its column's type.
  - The draft is still version 0: `commitVersion = 0` and `manifestVersion = 0`.

  **3. Core** (`internal/core`)
  - The cache table comes from `createSQL`: `create table main."t" ("v" int,
  primary key ("v")) strict, without rowid`.
  - `typeSQL` maps only `TYPE_INT` to `"int"`.
  - `load` refuses any `pragma_table_info` type other than `int` with
  `ErrCacheVersion`.
  - `table.checkValue` accepts only `Integer` in a `TYPE_INT` column, and NULL
  outside the key.
  - `bind` binds only `Integer` and `Null` ("a value of kind %v cannot be
  stored").
  - `readRow` reads only `TypeInteger` and `TypeNull`, and anything else gives
  `ErrCacheVersion`.
  - `CreateTable(*s4qlpb.CreateTable)`, `Upsert(name, []*s4qlpb.Value)`,
  `Delete` and `Get(name, key []*s4qlpb.Value)` and `Scan(name)` are all typed
  by `Value`.
  - SQLite orders the rows:
    - A scan is `select * … order by <key columns>`.
    - The commit's upserts and deletes are ordered by a `temp` table typed
  through `typeSQL`.
    - The unique lookup is `t."v" = ?1`.
  - `model_test.go` `compareKeys` uses `GetInteger()`, and `cacheDump` reads
  only `TypeInteger`.

  **4. SQL front end** (`internal/sql`)
  - The tokenizer already has `String`, `Blob` (`x'..'`), `Integer`, `Float` and
  `QNumber`, and the parser builds `ast.Literal{Value Token}` and
  `ast.TypeName{Names…}`.
  - No code decodes the value of a token: `token.go:39` says "Also left out: the
  value of a token".
  - `stmt/recognize.go` rejects:
    - a type other than the word `int` with "column type other than INT";
    - literals through `literalName`: "real number", "string", "blob", "NULL",
  "hex literal", "number with a digit separator";
    - a literal outside 64 bits with "integer outside 64 bits".
  - `stmt.Insert` holds `Value int64` or `Parameter int`, and `stmt.CreateTable`
  has no type field.

  **5. Engine** (`internal/engine`)
  - The plan has `Expr` = `*Integer{Value int64}` or `*Parameter`.
  - `NewPlan` hard-codes `Type_TYPE_INT`, and `typeName` handles only INT (it
  panics otherwise).
  - `eval` builds an `Integer` value.
  - `checkArguments` gives "argument %v, which is not an integer".
  - The unique check is `w.get(ctx, name, row)` in the core, so SQLite compares
  the keys. It reports `uniqueFailed` on column 0.
  - No code converts a value by its column's type.

  **6. Driver**
  - `conn.go` `argument` accepts only `int64` after
  `driver.DefaultParameterConverter`, which also passes `float64`, `string`,
  `[]byte`, `bool` and `time.Time`. Everything else is "not supported".
  - `rows.go` `value` already gives `int64`, `float64`, `string`, `[]byte` or
  nil.
  - Text, real and blob need cases in `argument`, a UTF-8 check on strings, and
  a NaN decision.

  **7. Tests**
  - `stmttest`: `Generator.Value func() int64`, `Integer(r)` and the sequence's
  values in -1..3 are integer-only. The generated `create table` uses `int`,
  `Others` lists `integer primary key` as not supported, and `Unsupported` lists
  non-integer arguments.
  - Engine differential test: `oracle.run(args ...int64)` binds only
  `BindInt64`, reads only `TypeInteger` and `TypeNull` (anything else is
  `Fatalf`), and `valueText` handles only Integer and Null.
  - `drivercheck`: compares `fmt.Sprintf("%T:%v")` of values scanned into `any`,
  which works for every type.
  - mattn v1.14.53:
    - Converts by declared type only for `timestamp`, `datetime`, `date` (to
  `time.Time`) and `boolean`. None of these occurs in a STRICT table.
    - It binds `time.Time` as text and `bool` as 1 or 0.
    - It binds a nil `[]byte` as NULL and reads an empty blob as `[]byte{}`.
  - Simulation: `answer` reads `GetInteger()`, the planted bug `annotateNext`
  adds 1 to the integer, and `stmtValue` handles every type.

  **8. SQLite 3.54.1, measured**

  I used a scratch test in the crosscheck module, now deleted, with two drivers:
  - the repository's binding, with `sqlite_version()` 3.54.1 and source ID
  `555e07ef…`;
  - mattn, with the same source ID, for the bound values.

  Every error is 19/3091 `cannot store X value in T column t.v` unless the cell
  says otherwise. NULL gives 19/1299 `NOT NULL constraint failed: t.v` in every
  column type. `INTEGER` behaves exactly like `INT`, and its message says
  `INTEGER column`.

  | value | int / integer | real | text | blob |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | 1 | integer 1 | real 1.0 | text '1' | INT err |
  | 9007199254740993 | integer 9007199254740993 | real 9007199254740992.0 | text
  '9007199254740993' | INT err |
  | 1.0 | integer 1 | real 1.0 | text '1.0' | REAL err |
  | 1.5 | REAL err | real 1.5 | text '1.5' | REAL err |
  | '1' | integer 1 | real 1.0 | text | TEXT err |
  | '1.0' | integer 1 | real 1.0 | text | TEXT err |
  | '1.5' | REAL err | real 1.5 | text | TEXT err |
  | 'abc' | TEXT err | TEXT err | text | TEXT err |
  | ' 1' | integer 1 | real 1.0 | text ' 1' | TEXT err |
  | '' | TEXT err | TEXT err | text '' | TEXT err |
  | x'31' / x'' | BLOB err | BLOB err | BLOB err | blob |
  | 1e999 / -1e999 | REAL err | real ±Inf (`9.0e+999`) | text 'Inf' / '-Inf' |
  REAL err |
  | bound NaN | 19/1299 NOT NULL | same | same | same |
  | -0.0 | integer 0 | real 0.0 (sign lost) | text '0.0' | REAL err |

  - Bound values behave as literals, with one exception: a bound float64 1 into
  a `text` column gives '1.0'.
  - 1 and 1.0 are the same key in `real`, `int` and `integer` columns (19/1555
  UNIQUE). So are 0.0 and −0.0 in either order, and even a bound −0 is stored
  with the sign bit clear.
  - Text keys come in BINARY order: `'' 'A' 'B' 'Z' 'a' 'aa' 'b' 'ä'`.
  - Blob keys come in memcmp order: `X'' X'00' X'42' X'61' X'6100' X'C3A4'
  X'FF'`.
  - Real keys come in numeric order: −Inf … +Inf.
  - `9223372036854775808` is real `9.2233720368547758e+18`. It fails in an INT
  column with a REAL error, is stored as that real in a REAL column, and as the
  text `'9.2233720368547758e+18'` in a TEXT column. `-9223372036854775808` is an
  integer.
  - `MAX_LENGTH=1000000000` (the default) and `MAX_SQL_LENGTH=1000000000`. One
  byte more gives 18/18 `string or blob too big`, for both literals and bound
  values.
  - Text with a NUL (`'a\0b'`) is stored as the bytes `610062`. `length()` gives
  1 and `quote()` gives `'a'`. `'a\0b'` and `'a\0c'` are two distinct keys.

  **9. What the format on S3 depends on**
  - Nothing in the commit object assumes an `int64` key: keys and rows are
  `repeated Value`, and their order comes from SQLite.
  - The only change to the format is new values of `Type`, which is allowed
  because the format is a draft.
  - `load`'s refusal of other types is about the local cache, not S3
  (`cacheVersion = 1`).

  **Layers in dependency order**
  1. **SQLite binding** (`internal/sqlite/sqlite.go` and `sqlite_browser.go`):
  small. It has no `BindDouble`, `BindBlob`, `ColumnDouble` or `ColumnBlob`. A
  change here needs a run under the address sanitizer.
  2. **Format**: small. New `Type` values, regenerated `commit.pb.go`, and the
  samples.
  3. **Core**: medium. `typeSQL`, `load`, `checkValue`, `bind`, `readRow`, and
  the model's `compareKeys`.
  4. **`stmt`**: large.
     - Accept the type words.
     - Decode strings (`''` for a quote) and hex blobs.
     - Parse reals exactly as SQLite does, including the case where an integer
  too large for 64 bits becomes a real.
     - Give `Insert` a typed value and update the README's vocabulary.
  5. **Engine**: large.
     - Literal nodes, `typeName`, and the argument checks.
     - SQLite's STRICT conversions on insert, which include writing reals as
  text exactly as SQLite does ('1.0', 'Inf', '9.2233720368547758e+18').
     - The datatype and NOT NULL errors.
  6. **Driver**: small. `argument` and its UTF-8 check. The rows code already
  handles every type.
  7. **Tests**: large. The generator, the differential oracle, the simulation's
  `answer` and `annotateNext`, the core model, and `internal/simulator`.

  **Design questions the documents leave open**
  - Does `integer` get its own `Type` value, or is it the same as `int`?
  (todo:207)
  - May a text value hold a NUL byte? (todo:32) SQLite stores one, and
  `length()` stops at it.
  - NaN: the format refuses it, and SQLite turns a bound NaN into NULL (the NOT
  NULL error). Should s4ql reproduce that, or reject the argument?
  - Infinity is allowed by the format and stored by SQLite, and no document
  rules on it.
  - −0.0 loses its sign in SQLite's real columns, while an annotation would keep
  −0 as given.
  - Should a value of another storage class be accepted where SQLite converts it
  ('1' into INT, 1 into REAL, 1.5 into TEXT)? The roadmap says both "reject,
  never reinterpret" and "behaves exactly as in SQLite".
  - Which Go argument types are accepted (`float64`, `string`, `[]byte`, nil,
  `bool`, `time.Time`)? Slice 2 rejects all of them today.
  - No rule bounds the size of a value: SQLite's limit is 1e9 bytes, and a
  commit object's is 5 GiB.
  - Rows come in BINARY order of their keys today. Whether that is promised
  without `ORDER BY` is still open (todo:34).


⏺ The research on types is back. The storage format already carries text, real
  and blob values, so slice 4 needs no new format beyond the type names. The
  work is in the statement pass, the engine, the core's cache tables and the
  tests.

  It also sharpens the question I asked. SQLite's conversions go further than
  one would guess: the real 1.5 goes into a text column as '1.5', the text ' 1'
  with a leading space goes into an int column as 1, and an integer above 2^53
  goes into a real column rounded. With my recommendation, only that last kind
  is kept (an integer into a real column, stored as SQLite stores it), and the
  rest is rejected.

  So, still open for you: exact types plus an integer into a real column, or
  everything SQLite converts?

  The lease fix is still being built.

  ## What I built

  **Core** (`internal/core`)
  - **Where the end happens:** `DB.endForLease` in `lease.go` runs at the start
  of `acquire` and `resume`, before the lease task waits for the writer lock.
  - **What it ends:** the open write transaction (`DB.writing`), unless that
  transaction is in `Commit` or `Rollback`. A transaction that is committing is
  left alone, so its manifest swap decides.
  - **The error:** every later call fails with `fmt.Errorf("%w: %v",
  ErrTxLeaseLost, reason)`. `ErrTxLeaseLost` is a new exported error that wraps
  `ErrNoLease`, with the text "the transaction was ended because the instance
  lost its lease". `Tx.Rollback` returns nil, and the new `Tx.Lost()` gives the
  error.
  - **Event:** `transaction_ended` at Info, or Warn if the roll back in the
  cache fails. Its attributes are `outcome`, `reason`, `commit.parent`,
  `transaction.waited_for_call` and `duration.total`.
  - **Planted bug:** `keepIdleTx`.

  **Engine**
  - `writeTx.lost()` is new.
  - A roll back of an ended transaction returns nil, and its `transaction` event
  takes the lease error.
  - `outcomeOf` gives the new outcome `lease_lost` at Debug, for that event and
  for statements refused that way.

  **Driver**
  - No code change. The error already matches only `ErrNotServing`, and the roll
  back already returns nil.
  - I added two sentences to the section "Transactions" of the package
  documentation in `s4ql.go`.

  **Documents**
  - In `docs/todo.md`, the open item under "Transactions" is now the decision,
  with my decisions of detail.
  - The READMEs of the core and the engine describe the behaviour and the tests.
  - I also changed one entry in `internal/sql/mutation/equivalent.txt`. The
  mutant of `ending.end` changed with the code, and `TestEquivalent` failed
  until the entry named it again.

  ## How a running call and open rows are ordered against the end

  - **The lease task never uses the write connection while a call of the
  transaction does.** It marks the transaction under `DB.mu`, in the same
  critical section where every call counts itself on the connection (`Tx.use`).
    - If no call runs, the lease task rolls back and frees the writer lock at
  once.
    - Otherwise the last call to leave the connection does it (`Tx.leave`).
  - **A call that runs is not cut.** It finishes with its own result. Calls that
  start after the mark fail.
  - **A scan checks the mark at each row** (an `atomic.Bool`, `Tx.marked`), so a
  loop over a scan stops at its next row with the error.
  - **Rows of a scan that nobody reads hold the writer lock** until the caller
  reads the next row (which gives the error) or stops the scan. The scan holds a
  SQLite statement on the write connection, and no other goroutine can end that
  safely. This is the one case where the instance still does not serve again by
  itself. Through `database/sql`, the rows also end when the context of their
  query ends.
  - **No transaction can begin unseen.** `DB.Begin` records its transaction and
  gives back the connection in one critical section that checks `usableLocked`.

  ## Decisions of detail
  - Only the waits in `acquire` and `resume` end a transaction. A lease that has
  only run out by the clocks ends nothing, because a renewal needs no writer
  lock.
  - `resume` cannot meet an idle transaction today: it only runs after a
  commit's own unknown outcome. It still calls `endForLease`, as you asked.
  - `Tx.state` checks done, then the lease end, then failed. So the lease error
  wins over "can only be rolled back", and after Commit or Rollback a call gives
  `ErrTxDone`.
  - The engine's `transaction.end` stays `commit` or `rollback`, meaning what
  the application called. The outcome `lease_lost` and the error say how the
  transaction actually ended.
  - The core logs no `refusal` event for calls refused because the transaction
  was ended; `transaction_ended` covers them.

  ## Simulations and run times
  - **Engine:** a new workload `idle` (the default workload plus one idler per
  instance, `run.idler`).
    - Each idler runs two write transactions that sit idle while the idler cuts
  its own instance off from the store, often for long enough that the other
  instance takes the lease. The second is cut off for 2 minutes, idles for 3,
  and stays open past the end of the workload.
    - `run.readsFree` closes instances in turn and now gives its own violation
  for an instance that still has that transaction: "...is open and idle, does
  not serve...".
    - The core's `transaction_ended` event is fed into `run.verifyOverlaps`,
  because the core frees the writer's place before the caller ends the
  transaction.
    - 1,000 seeds: 1,348 transactions ended for the lease, 1,317 of them kept
  open, no violation. Both counts are pinned at 0.33 per seed.
  - **Engine bug check, by hand:** the engine cannot plant a core bug, so I made
  `endForLease` do nothing and restored it after. 89 of the first 100 seeds
  failed, all by the new violation.
  - **Core:** a new workload `idling` (`competing` plus idlers, 20 seeds), with
  `run.verifyIdle`. It closes every other live instance and requires the
  instance whose transaction is still open to serve within `workload.liveness`.
    - The planted bug `keepIdleTx` was found in 97 of 200 seeds, by "is open and
  idle, does not serve".
    - Its control (one instance, so the lease is never lost) found it in 0 of
  200, and the workload without the bug found nothing in 0 of 200.
    - `idleReaches` pins a quarter of what the 20 seeds reached, including 5 of
  "event transaction_ended/rolled_back".
  - **Run times**, binaries from 74a7ac8 and from the branch, run in turns, at a
  load of 12 to 19:

  | Test | Before | After |
  |---|---|---|
  | engine `TestSimulation` | 3.84 s, 3.49 s | 5.45 s, 5.17 s |
  | core `TestSimulation` + `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` | 41.04 s, 40.65 s |
  43.64 s, 43.08 s |

  ## Where this differs from the brief

  ## Self-review

⏺ The lease fix is built and in pull request #66. CI is running, and an
  independent reviewer is checking it before I merge, since it changes the lease
  logic in the core.

  What it does: when the instance needs to take the lease and a write
  transaction is open and not committing, the core rolls it back and frees the
  writer's place. Later statements and the commit give ErrNotServing, and
  tx.Rollback gives nil. In 1,000 simulated seeds, 1,348 transactions were ended
  this way with no violation, and the instance served again each time.

  Two things I'm having the reviewer judge:

  - One gap remains. If a query's rows are open and nobody reads them, the
    transaction can't be ended from outside until the application reads or
    closes them. Through database/sql they also end when the query's context
    ends.
  - A lease that only lapses by the clock and is renewed by the same instance
    doesn't end the transaction. The builder says that's safe because no other
    instance held the lease in between. I want that checked against the spec
    sentence you just agreed to.

✻ Baked for 49s · done 8:14 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  **Verdict: sound to merge.** I found no safety, lock or race fault. Two things
  need action: the spec sentence (finding 1), and one existing test that lost
  its point (finding 2). The worktree is clean; the scratch test and the mutants
  I ran there have been removed.

  **1. A lease that runs out only by the clocks does not end the transaction,
  and the spec says it should (`internal/core/core.go:536`,
  `internal/core/lease.go:323,482`).** I proved this with a scratch test on the
  synctest clock:
  - a serves, `Begin`, `Upsert(1)`.
  - All swaps and gets are dropped for 45 s. `serves` is false.
  - During that time `Upsert(2)` and `Get` succeed. `tx.Serving` gives
  `lease_expired_monotonic` and `tx.Lost()` is nil.
  - The store comes back and the lease is renewed. `Upsert(3)` succeeds, and
  `Commit` returns nil.
  - The log then holds one commit with rows 1, 2 and 3.

  So the statements never fail, and the transaction is usable afterwards. This
  is because `usableLocked` ignores the clocks on purpose, and holder and
  serving stay true.

  Committing afterwards is safe:
  - Another instance can take the lease only with a swap. That changes the
  version, and a version with a lease entry never comes back.
  - Any later renewal or commit of a is sent from `d.version`, so it is refused.
  `observe` then calls `lose`, the next try goes through `acquire`, and
  `endForLease` ends the transaction.
  - During a partition, b can take over and commit while a still believes it
  holds the lease. a's transaction then goes on accepting statements, but it can
  never publish.

  This does not match the sentence. "An instance without a valid lease" includes
  one whose lease ran out by the clocks, and for that case the spec says the
  transaction "fails: it is rolled back".

  **The sentence should give way, not the code.** Its own reason is "so that it
  does not keep the instance from getting the lease again", and that does not
  apply here: renewal needs no writer lock. The rule just before it says "Writes
  are never affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap." Reads inside the
  transaction come from its begin snapshot, and a commit of nothing is checked
  against the clocks. Ending the transaction on clock expiry would kill
  transactions on every slow renewal (more than 10 s late with the defaults) or
  monotonic stall, and gain no safety.

  Suggested wording, which needs Markus's agreement: "A write transaction that
  is open and not committing fails when the instance finds that it has lost the
  lease or does not serve; one whose lease has only run out by the clocks goes
  on, and its commit is decided by the manifest swap."

  **2. `TestLease_waiting`, the subtest "…finds that it has lost the lease"
  (`internal/core/lease_test.go:844-864`), no longer tests what it was written
  for.** Its comment says "only the news of its loss ends the wait". Now
  `endForLease` frees the writer lock, so the waiter's semaphore acquire
  succeeds and `servingLocked` fails anyway. I proved it: with `d.wakeLocked()`
  removed from `lose`, the test still passes. If I add `o.Plant("keepIdleTx")`
  to its options, the same mutant fails at line 860 and the real code passes.
  That is the fix: plant `keepIdleTx` there, and the original `l.publish()` can
  then come back.

  **3. Unread scan rows (the remaining gap).** This is the smallest gap with a
  scan that keeps a SQLite statement open between rows. Ending that statement
  from the lease task's goroutine would need concurrent use of the connection.

  While the gap lasts:
  - the lease task is blocked in `acquireWriter(work)`
  - the instance holds no lease and refuses everything
  - other instances can take the lease normally
  - `Close` cancels the wait
  - `database/sql` ends such rows when the query's context ends

  The only design that closes the gap is a scan that holds no statement between
  rows. It would seek again by the last primary key for each row or batch, which
  is valid because the transaction may not change the table it scans. It is not
  worth doing now; it could be recorded as an option in `docs/todo.md`.

  **What I checked and found sound:**
  - **Mark versus a call starting or ending:** `endForLease` (`lease.go:452`),
  `Tx.use` and `Tx.useToEnd` decide in the same critical section under `DB.mu`.
  A call either has registered `users` before the mark, or it fails. A `Commit`
  either set `ending` first (and its swap decides) or it fails with
  `ErrTxLeaseLost`.
  - **Rollback before catch-up:** the cache transaction is always rolled back
  before the writer lock is freed. In the idle case the lease task does it
  itself, holding `users++` so that `Close` cannot close the connection under
  it. Otherwise the last `leave` does it, after the scan's iterator has finished
  its statement.
  - **Writer lock released exactly once:** checked on every path. `Tx.end`
  returns early when `lease != nil`; `leave` returns after `endForLease`, which
  does the one `unuse`. A panic goes through the deferred `leave`. A stop before
  the mark skips the mark, and a stop after it still releases.
  - **No new transaction slips in:** `Begin` records `writing` in the same
  critical section that requires serving. `endForLease` only runs when the
  instance does not serve, and only the lease task itself, or a committing
  transaction (which is skipped), can make it serve again.
  - **Locks:** `endForLease` holds no lock while it rolls back or releases, so
  there is no lock-order problem with `DB.mu`.
  - **Theoretical only:** if the rollback in `Tx.endForLease` fails with an
  error that is not local, the cache transaction could stay open. SQLite does
  not fail that way in practice.

  **Tests:**
  - Two mutants are caught by `TestLease_openTx`: dropping the rollback in
  `Tx.endForLease`, and dropping the per-row mark check in `Scan`.
  - The tests run on synctest's fake clock and do not depend on timing.
  - `go test ./internal/core -short -race -count=3 -run 'TestLease|TestTx'`
  passes, and so does the engine's `TestConn_leaseLost` with `-race -count=2`.
  - The planted bug `keepIdleTx` and the `idling` and `idle` workloads check
  only that the instance serves again. Safety is covered by the unit tests
  checking the cache and the log, plus the simulation's usual checks of the
  cache against the log.

⏺ Agent "Review lease ends transaction" finished · 6m 25s

⏺ The review found the lease fix sound: nothing of an ended transaction can
  reach the log, and the writer's place is freed exactly once on every path. A
  small builder is doing its three follow-ups (one test that lost its point, a
  test for the case below, and a todo entry).

  One finding needs you, because it touches the sentence you agreed to an hour
  ago.

  A lease that only runs out by the clock does not end the transaction.

  If the instance is slow to renew and no other instance took the lease, the
  open transaction is kept. After the renewal it goes on and can commit. The
  reviewer showed that this is safe: another instance can only take the lease
  with a manifest swap, and after such a swap this instance's commit is always
  refused.

  The spec sentence says the transaction is rolled back whenever the instance
  has no valid lease, which is stricter than the code. The reviewer recommends
  that the sentence give way, and I agree: ending transactions on every slow
  renewal would cost applications work and gain no safety.

  Proposed wording, replacing the sentence from before:

  "A commit that is under way succeeds only if its manifest swap still succeeds.
  When the instance finds that it has lost the lease, a write transaction that
  is open and not committing fails: it is rolled back, and its statements and
  its commit get the same temporary error, so that it does not keep the instance
  from getting the lease again. A write transaction of an instance whose lease
  has only run out by the clocks is not rolled back: its statements fail in the
  same way until the lease is renewed, and its commit is decided by the manifest
  swap."

  Okay to write it so? I'm having the middle part of the last sentence
  (statements fail until the renewal) pinned by a test first, and will tell you
  if the code does something else.

  transaction gives the writer lock to the waiter, which then fails anyway.
  - **Check with `d.wakeLocked()` removed from `lose`:** the test fails with
  `lease_test.go:862: Expected "1", but got "0" (type int)`. The waiter is never
  woken.

  and unchanged.

  **2. A lease that only runs out by the clocks**

  New subtest in the core, in `TestLease_openTx`: "should keep an open write
  transaction when the lease only runs out by the clocks, and commit it after
  the renewal". It uses the fake clock in a bubble and a script store that drops
  every swap and get for 45 seconds, with a 30-second lease and no other
  instance.
  - **At 35 seconds:** the instance does not serve. `Tx.Serving` gives
  `ErrNoLease` with a `lease_expired…` reason. `Tx.Upsert` still works, because
  the other calls of an open transaction do not look at the clocks.
  - **After the store comes back:** the instance serves within 5 seconds and
  `Tx.Lost()` stays nil, so `endForLease` never ran. Then `Tx.Serving` is nil, a
  third upsert works, and the commit succeeds.
  - **The log:** the create commit, then one commit with exactly `upsert t1 [0]
  1, upsert t1 [0] 2, upsert t1 [0] 3`.

  New subtest in the engine, in `TestConn_leaseLost`
  (`internal/engine/tx_test.go`). It cuts client "a" off for 45 seconds with
  `CutOff`, and no other instance runs. Exactly what the engine does:
  - **During the lapse (at 35 seconds):** both statements I ran in the open
  transaction fail: `insert into t1 (v1) values (3)` through `Exec`, and `select
  * from t1` through `Query`. A third statement, `insert into t1 (v1) values
  (4)`, fails the same way. The error text is `no valid lease:
  lease_expired_monotonic`. It matches `core.ErrNoLease`, the temporary error,
  and does not match `core.ErrTxLeaseLost`.
  - **The transaction is not rolled back.** After `d.Wait` returns (the
  renewal), `select` in the transaction gives `rows 1`. So the inserts that
  failed had no effect.
  - **After the renewal:** `insert into t1 (v1) values (2)` succeeds, `Commit`
  succeeds, and the table holds `rows 1 2`.
  - **The log** (`logAnnotations`) holds `[[create table t1 (v1 int primary key)
  strict, without rowid] [insert into t1 (v1) values (1) insert into t1 (v1)
  values (2)]]`. That is exactly the statements that succeeded.

  **3. Documents**

  now says that a lease that only runs out by the clocks keeps the transaction,
  what happens meanwhile in the core and the engine, and which tests pin it.
  - **`docs/todo.md`, "Not built" of that section:** the known gap is a new
  item. Rows of a scan that nobody reads keep the writer's place until the
  caller reads the next row or stops the scan, because the scan holds a SQLite
  statement on the write connection between rows. Meanwhile the instance holds
  no lease and refuses everything, other instances can take the lease, and
  `Close` ends the wait. A scan that seeks again by the last key for each row or
  batch would close the gap, and is not built.
  - **`internal/core/README.md`:** the two bullets on the end for the lease say
  the same in short.
  - `docs/spec.md` is not touched.

  **Self-review:** a `/code-review` at the low level found nothing in the two
  documents and left out the test files, so I checked the test logic myself. The
  tests use only the fake clock inside the bubble: the deadline of `Wait` is
  fake time, so it does not depend on the machine. None of them skips. The diary
  has a new step 6 in `docs/diary/2026-11-19-lease-ends-transaction.md`.

✻ Brewed for 27s · done 8:40 PM

❯ One at a time please                                                          

⏺ The spec sentence on a lost lease needs one more change, because the code is
  gentler than what you agreed to: a transaction survives when the lease only
  lapses by the clock and the same instance renews it.

  Proposed, replacing the sentence from earlier:

  "A commit that is under way succeeds only if its manifest swap still succeeds.
  When the instance finds that it has lost the lease, a write transaction that
  is open and not committing fails: it is rolled back, and its statements and
  its commit get the same temporary error, so that it does not keep the instance
  from getting the lease again. A write transaction of an instance whose lease
  has only run out by the clocks is not rolled back: its statements fail in the
  same way until the lease is renewed, and its commit is decided by the manifest
  swap."

  Okay to write it so?

✻ Crunched for 2s · done 9:02 PM

❯ Don't understand it.                                                          

⏺ There are two ways an instance can be without a valid lease, and an open
  transaction is treated differently in each.

  1. Another instance took the lease. The transaction is dead. It can never
  commit, so the instance rolls it back at once. This is what you agreed to
  earlier.

  2. The lease just ran out on the clock. For example S3 was slow for 40
  seconds, so the instance could not renew in time. No other instance took over.
  Here the transaction is kept:

  - While the lease is expired, its statements fail with the "not serving"
    error.
  - When the instance renews the lease, the transaction works again and can
    commit.

  Case 2 is safe because nobody else wrote in between. If someone had, the
  commit would be refused by S3.

  The sentence I wrote into the spec earlier says the transaction is rolled back
  in both cases. The code only does that in case 1. I want the spec to describe
  both cases as they are.

  Do you agree with keeping the transaction in case 2? If yes, I'll write the
  spec so. If you'd rather have it rolled back there too, the code changes
  instead.
✻ Churned for 4s · done 9:03 PM

❯ And we're absolute sure it's safe because of the compare and swap right?      

⏺ Yes. What a transaction writes is safe because of the compare-and-swap alone,
  and no clock is involved in that.

  - Every commit is a swap of the manifest from the version the instance last
    saw.
  - Another instance can only take the lease with such a swap, and that changes
    the version.
  - So if anyone else took over, this instance's commit is refused by S3. That
    holds even if they released the lease again afterwards.

  The renewal is a swap of the same kind. If it succeeds, nobody else touched
  the database in between, so what the transaction read is still the newest
  state. If it fails, the instance has lost the lease, and that is case 1: the
  transaction is rolled back.

  What a transaction reads while the lease is expired is guarded by the clocks,
  as every other read is. Each statement checks the lease first and fails during
  the gap.

  It's tested: one test in the core and one in the engine run exactly this
  (lease lapses, is renewed, the transaction commits, the log holds exactly its
  statements), and the reviewer traced the takeover paths.

  Okay to write the spec so?

✻ Cogitated for 14s · done 9:05 PM

      41  - **The holder keeps the lease.** While the holder keeps renewi
          ng the lease, no other instance can take it. Another instance g
          ets the lease only after the holder has released it or it has t
          imed out.
      42  - **The lease times out when the manifest stands still.** The m
          anifest records how long the lease lasts, and every renewal cha
          nges it by writing a new random value. Another instance takes t
          he lease only after it has watched the manifest stay unchanged
          for longer than that, by its own monotonic clock. No instance c
          ompares its clock with another's. An instance that starts after
           a crash therefore waits at least one lease duration before it
          serves.
      43  - **Clocks affect only reads.** Before a read, the holder check
          s that less than the lease's duration has passed since it sent
          its last renewal, by its wall clock and by its monotonic clock,
           and both must show it. If an instance measures time wrongly, a
           read can miss the commits of a newer holder. Writes are never
          affected, because S3 checks the manifest swap.
      44 -- **An instance without a valid lease refuses and keeps trying.
         -** Reads and new write transactions fail at once with a tempora
         -ry error. A commit that is under way succeeds only if its manif
         -est swap still succeeds. A write transaction that is open and n
         -ot committing fails: it is rolled back, and its statements and 
         -its commit get the same temporary error, so that it does not ke
         -ep the instance from getting the lease again. The instance keep
         -s trying to get the lease, catches up its cache when it gets it
         -, and then serves again. This holds at startup, when S3 cannot 
         -be reached, and while another instance holds the lease, so a sp
         -are instance is a normal state.

      45  - **Opening does not wait for the lease.** Opening a database f
          ails only on local problems. A separate call waits until the in
          stance serves.
      46  - **Reads come from the local cache.** While an instance holds
          the lease, it reads from its local cache without contacting S3.
           Writes go to S3.
      47  - **The local cache is a SQLite database.** It holds the same t

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's in the spec (ca55d50, pushed). The spec and the code now agree on both
  cases.

  Next one, for slice 4 (the other types): which values does a column take that
  are not of its own type?

  SQLite's STRICT tables still convert on insert. The text '1' goes into an int
  column as 1, and the real 1.5 goes into a text column as '1.5'.

  1. Exact types, plus an integer into a real column (my recommendation). int
     takes integers, text takes text, blob takes blobs, real takes reals and
     integers. Everything else is rejected. It can be loosened later without
     breaking anyone.
  2. Everything SQLite converts. Fully compatible, but it brings back
     string-to-number guessing, and s4ql would have to reproduce SQLite's number
     parsing and float printing bit for bit.

✻ Sautéed for 11s · done 9:05 PM

❯ 1, but maybe not even int to float? Let's stay strict and maybe loosen later  

⏺ Noted: exact types only. An integer into a real column is rejected too, so
  values (1) for a real column must be written 1.0 or passed as a float64.

  Next one: may a text value hold a NUL byte?

  This is listed as open in the todo. SQLite stores such a value, but its own
  functions stop at the NUL: length('a\0b') gives 1, and quote shows 'a'. So a
  stored value and what SQL says about it disagree.

  My recommendation: reject it. A text argument with a NUL byte is "not
  supported", in line with the rule that already rejects a NUL byte in SQL text.
  Binary data belongs in a blob, which takes any bytes.

  Agree?

⏺ Slice 4 has started with its smallest piece: the SQLite binding learns to bind
  and read reals and blobs. The storage core and the statement pass follow on
  top of it, in parallel, then the engine and driver, then the generated checks.

  What I decided for the slice beyond your two answers, all in the roadmap:

  - A wrong type is always "not supported", also where SQLite would give its own
    error (text into a blob column). One rule, no guessing which case it is.
  - int and integer stay distinct in the commit format, so a replay declares the
    column as it was declared.
  - Go arguments: integers, float64, string, and []byte. Still rejected: nil,
    bool, time.Time, and a nil []byte.
  - NaN and infinity are rejected. SQLite silently stores a NaN as NULL.
  - -0.0 is stored as 0.0, as SQLite does, and both are the same key.
  - Real literals must give the same 64-bit float as SQLite, bit for bit. A
    literal where that isn't sure is rejected.

  your 90%, so I'll stop there and go on after the reset on Friday 21:00, unless
  you raise the limit.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Both bindings (cgo and browser) can now bind and read reals and blobs, and
  every check you listed passed. The work is on branch `binding-types`, three
  commits on top of `origin/main` at `2724cfc`, in
  /Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a9eee1e623fffe049. Not
  pushed, no pull request.

  **Functions added, in /internal/sqlite/sqlite.go and
  /internal/sqlite/sqlite_browser.go, same signatures in both:**
  - `BindDouble(i int, v float64) error` and `ColumnDouble(i int) float64`.
  Every bit pattern comes through `select ?`, including -0.0 and both
  infinities.
  - `BindBlob(i int, v []byte) error`:
    - Native: through a new static C helper `s4ql_bind_blob`. It works like
  `s4ql_bind_text`: a null pointer becomes `""`, and it calls
  `sqlite3_bind_blob64` with `SQLITE_TRANSIENT`.
    - Browser: copies the blob into SQLite's memory first. That allocation is
  one byte longer than the blob, so its address is never 0, even for the empty
  blob.
    - It fails with `SQLITE_TOOBIG` above the connection's length limit.
  - `ColumnBlob(i int) []byte` returns a copy that stays valid after the next
  step. NULL reads as nil. An empty blob, or an empty text, reads as an empty
  slice that is not nil.
  - The `Type` constants for real and blob already existed.
  - One addition you didn't ask for: the doc comment of `SetLengthLimit` now
  says SQLite raises any limit below 30 to 30. A test with a limit of 10 failed
  because of this.

  **`BindText` and `ColumnText` with a NUL byte or the empty string:** they were
  already correct and I changed nothing. `BindText` passes the length and maps
  a null pointer to `""`, and `ColumnText` reads by length (`C.GoStringN`
  natively). The existing tests already covered `"a\x00b"`, `"\x00"` and `""` in
  both bindings and in the cross-check.

  **What a bound NaN does:** SQLite binds it as NULL, so `typeof(?)` is `null`.
  mattn does the same, and the tests check it.

  **What a nil slice does:** I decided `BindBlob(i, nil)` binds the empty blob,
  the same as `[]byte{}`. That way `BindBlob` always binds a blob, and
  `BindNull` is the only way to bind NULL. mattn binds nil as NULL, so the
  cross-check's fuzz test turns nil into `[]byte{}` before comparing.

  **Things later builders should know:**
  - **Browser doubles:** they pass as 8 bytes through the transfer array, using
  two new helpers in /internal/sqlite/browser/bridge.js. `syscall/js` turns -0.0
  into 0 in both directions (`floatValue` in `syscall/js/js.go`, `storeValue`
  in `wasm_exec.js`), so passing a Number would lose the sign. The simulator's
  worker loads `bridge.js` too.
  - **-0.0 in a table:** in a `real` column of a strict table, -0.0 comes back
  as 0.0. SQLite stores a real with no fraction as an integer in the record.
  This is SQLite's behaviour, and the tests assert it. `select ?` and `select
  -0.0` keep the sign.

  **Tests:**
  - /internal/sqlite/sqlite_test.go:
    - `TestStmt_BindDouble`, `TestStmt_BindBlob`, `TestStmt_ColumnDouble`,
  `TestStmt_ColumnBlob`: round trips through `select ?` and a strict table,
  `ColumnType` and `typeof()`, edge values, a 4 MiB blob, conversions, and
  panics.
    - `FuzzStmt_BindDouble` and `FuzzStmt_BindBlob`.
    - The test helper `rows` now encodes reals, texts and blobs.
  - Two browser tests in /internal/sqlite/sqlite_browser_test.go.
  - /internal/sqlite/crosscheck/crosscheck_test.go: `TestBindDouble`,
  `FuzzBindDouble`, `TestBindBlob`, `FuzzBindBlob`, plus scripts reading reals
  and blobs from SQL and from strict tables. Both sides encode reals with their
  bits, so -0.0 and 0.0 differ.
  - CI's fuzz shards get four more fuzz tests.

  **Checks run, all passing:**
  - `make test` and `make lint` (simlint included).
  - `make check-browser`.
  - Browser tests in headless Chromium, which do run on this machine: `make
  test-browser BROWSERPKGS=./internal/sqlite`, also with `-count=20 -run
  TestStmt_Bind`.
  - The new tests with `-count=20`, with and without `-race`.
  - The cross-check's `TestBind*` tests with `-count=20 -race`.
  - The sanitizer in the `golang` Docker container: `go test -asan -short
  -shuffle on ./internal/sqlite/...`, before and after the review fix. The run
  before the fix also included the cross-check's `TestBind` tests.

  **Self-review (code-review skill, medium):**
  - Fixed: `ColumnBlob` used the connection's error code to tell an empty blob
  from out of memory. Another call on the same connection can leave a NOMEM
  there after the step, so an empty blob could panic. It now decides from the
  value itself (length above 0, or the value is a number), in both bindings.
  - Left, because they don't matter in practice:
    - In the browser, a blob over about 1 GB panics when the copy can't be
  allocated, where the cgo build returns `SQLITE_TOOBIG`.
    - In the browser, the `SQLITE_TOOBIG` above `math.MaxInt32` can carry the
  connection's last message.
    - `BindText` has the same patterns in both cases.

  **Follow-up:** `ColumnText`, in both bindings, still reads the connection's
  error code to tell the empty text from out of memory. The `ColumnBlob` fix
  would suit it too. I left it because it is outside this task.

  **Other files changed:**
  - /internal/sqlite/README.md and /internal/sqlite/browser/README.md.
  - The diary, at /docs/diary/2026-11-19-binding-types.md.
